Proof of the Landau-Pekar Formula for the effective Mass of the Polaron at strong coupling
Morris Brooks

Abstract
. We study the Fröhlich polaron in the regime of strong coupling and prove the asymptotically sharp lower bound on the effective mass m eff ​ ( α ) ≥ α 4 ​ m LP − C ​ α 4 − ϵ m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\geq\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}-C\alpha^{4-\epsilon} , where m LP m_{\mathrm{LP}} is an explicit constant. Together with the corresponding upper bound, which has been verified recently in [ 5 ] , we confirm the validity of the celebrated Landau-Pekar formula [ 12 ] from 1948 for the effective mass lim α → ∞ ​ α − 4 ​ m eff ​ ( α ) = m LP \underset{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\alpha^{-4}m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)=m_{\mathrm{LP}} as conjectured by Spohn [ 25 ] in 1987.



1.
Introduction and Main Result

In this manuscript we study the Fröhlich polaron, which is a model describing the interactions of a charged particle, e.g. an electron, with a polarizable medium
[
10
]
. From a mathematical point of view, the Fröhlich polaron is a popular toy model of a quantum field theory, as it is simple enough to allow for rigorous mathematical proofs while still giving rise to complex and non-trivial phenomena, such as the effective increase of the electrons mass due to its interactions with the quantized excitations of the medium. It will be the objective of this article to study the dependence of the effective mass
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)
on the coupling strength
\alpha
as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
goes to infinity. Due to a conjecture by Spohn
[
25
]
, it is expected that
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)
grows with a quartic power in
\alpha
according to the Landau-Pekar formula
[
12
]
, i.e. there exists a (rather explicit) constant
m_{\mathrm{LP}}\in(0,\infty)
such that

(1.1)

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\alpha^{-4}m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)=m_{\mathrm{LP}}.

Making use of the creation and annihilation operators
a^{*}
and
a
acting on the Fock space
\mathcal{F}
over
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
, which satisfy for
f,g\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
the re-scaled canonical commutation relations

\displaystyle[a(f),a^{*}(g)]=\alpha^{-2}\langle f,g\rangle,

we introduce the Fröhlich Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}
as the self-adjoint operator

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}:=-\Delta_{x}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v_{x})-a(v_{x})

acting on the Hilbert space
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}
, where
v_{x}(y):=\pi^{-\frac{3}{2}}|y-x|^{-2}
and
\mathcal{N}
is the (re-scaled) particle number operator defined in terms of an orthonormal basis
\{u_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
of
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
as

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a^{*}(u_{j})a(u_{j}).

Note that
\Delta_{x}
refers to the Laplace operator acting only on the
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
factor in
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}
and
x
refers to the position of the electron, i.e. the position operator in
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
. A detailed introduction to Fock space formalism and the objects appearing in the definition of
\mathbb{H}
in Eq. (
1.2
) is given in Section
2
. It is a central observation that
\mathbb{H}
is invariant under a joint translation of the electron
x\mapsto x-z
and the polarization field
a(f)\mapsto a(f_{z})
, where we define the shifted function
f_{z}(y):=f(y-z)
, which is generated by a family of self-adjoint operators

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}=(\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{3}).

Consequently, the joint spectrum
\sigma(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{H})
is well-defined and we can introduce the ground state energy as a function of the total momentum
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
as

\displaystyle E_{\alpha}(p):=\inf\{E\in\mathbb{R}:(p,E)\in\sigma(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{H})\}.

It is known that
E_{\alpha}(p)
obtains its global minimum at
p=0
, see
[
7
]
, and therefore the ground state energy
E_{\alpha}:=\inf\sigma(\mathbb{H})
is given by
E_{\alpha}(0)
. In the past decades numerous results have been obtained on the asymptotic behaviour of
E_{\alpha}
and
E_{\alpha}(p)
in the regime of strong coupling, such as the seminal work
[
6
]
, which established the convergence of the ground state energy

(1.4)

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}E_{\alpha}=e^{\mathrm{Pek}}

to the minimal value
e^{\mathrm{Pek}}
of the corresponding semi-classical functional

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi):=\|\varphi\|^{2}+\inf\sigma\Big(\!-\Delta-2v*\mathfrak{Re}[\varphi]\Big),

where
v(y):=\pi^{-\frac{3}{2}}|y|^{-2}
and
\varphi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
. Notably the proof of Eq. (
1.4
) in
[
6
]
is based on a functional integral representation of
E_{\alpha}
using a Feynman-Kac formula for the semi-group
e^{-T\mathbb{H}}
, see
[
9
]
. Later, an elementary proof of Eq. (
1.4
) has been established in
[
17
]
using a functional analytic approach. Regarding the asymptotic behaviour of the energy-momentum relation
E_{\alpha}(p)
and the effective mass
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)
, where

(1.6)

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha):=\underset{P\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\frac{|p|^{2}}{2(E_{\alpha}(p)-E_{\alpha})},

there has been significant progress in recent years. It has been verified in
[
15
]
that

(1.7)

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty,

i.e. the effective mass diverges in the limit of large
\alpha
. Using a functional integral representation of
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)
, see
[
25
,
7
]
and
[
22
,
2
]
, the rate of divergence in Eq. (
1.7
) has been quantified in
[
2
]
by a lower bound of the form
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\geq c\alpha^{\frac{2}{5}}
, which has been improved in
[
24
]
by the almost quartic lower bound
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\geq c(\log\alpha)^{-6}\alpha^{4}
and in
[
1
]
by a lower bound of the form

(1.8)

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\geq c\alpha^{4}

for a suitable constant
c\in(0,\infty)
. It is worth pointing out, that Eq. (
1.8
) already captures the correct quartic divergence
\alpha^{4}
of the effective mass as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
. In our main Theorem
1.1
we are going to improve Eq. (
1.8
) by establishing the lower bound

(1.9)

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\geq\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}-C\alpha^{4-\epsilon}

for a suitable
\epsilon>0
, where
m_{\mathrm{LP}}:=\frac{2}{3}\left\|\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right\|^{2}
and
\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}>0
is the unique radial minimizer of the Pekar functional defined in Eq. (
1.5
), see
[
13
]
. The corresponding upper bound

(1.10)

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\leq\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}+C\alpha^{4-\epsilon}

has been proven recently in
[
5
]
, using the results in
[
23
]
. Therefore we conclude that the constant
m_{\mathrm{LP}}
in Eq. (
1.9
) is the optimal one such that an inequality of the form Eq. (
1.9
) holds. Combining Eq. (
1.9
), see Theorem
1.1
, and Eq. (
1.10
) confirms the Landau-Pekar-Spohn conjecture on the effective mass stated in Eq. (
1.1
).

Regarding the upper bound in Eq. (
1.10
), it has been shown in
[
5
]
, using an upper bound on
E_{\alpha}
from
[
20
]
, that the energy-momentum relation
E_{\alpha}(p)
satisfies a lower bound of the form

(1.11)

\displaystyle E_{\alpha}(p)-E_{\alpha}\geq\min\left\{\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}},\alpha^{-2}\right\}-C\alpha^{-(2+\epsilon)}.

Since the error term
C\alpha^{-(2+\epsilon)}
does not scale like
|p|^{2}
in
p
, this lower bound alone is clearly insufficient to obtain a non-trivial upper bound on
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)
. However, together with the results in
[
23
]
, which especially imply that
E_{\alpha}(p)\leq E_{\alpha}+\frac{|p|^{2}}{2m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)}
, Eq. (
1.10
) follows from Eq. (
1.11
). A corresponding upper bound to Eq. (
1.11
) has been verified previously in
[
20
]

(1.12)

\displaystyle E_{\alpha}(p)-E_{\alpha}\leq\min\left\{\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}},\alpha^{-2}\right\}+C\alpha^{-\left(\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon\right)},

using a lower bound on
E_{\alpha}
from
[
4
]
. Again, due to the lack of a
|p|^{2}
scaling in the error term
C\alpha^{-\left(\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon\right)}
, Eq. (
1.12
) alone is insufficient to obtain a non-trivial lower bound on
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
m_{\mathrm{LP}}
be the Landau-Pekar constant introduced below Eq. (
1.9
) and
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)
the effective mass defined in Eq. (
1.6
). Then there exist
C,\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\geq\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}-C\alpha^{4-\epsilon}.

Proof strategy of Theorem
1.1
.
By the definition of the effective mass
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)
in Eq. (
1.6
), it is clearly enough to show the upper bound

(1.13)

\displaystyle E_{\alpha}(p)\leq E_{\alpha}+\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}+C\alpha^{-(4+\epsilon)}|p|^{2}.

In order to find a more convenient expression for
E_{\alpha}(p)
, let us switch to the Lee-Low-Pines picture,
[
11
]
, where the frame for the polarization field moves along with the position of the electron
x
. After applying this unitary transformation, the operator
\mathbb{H}
reads

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}=\left(\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v)-a(v),

where
\mathcal{P}
is the generator of the translations
a(f)\mapsto a(f_{z})
and
v(y):=\pi^{-\frac{3}{2}}|y|^{-2}
, and we define for
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
the fiber Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}_{p}
acting only on the Fock space
\mathcal{F}
as

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{p}:=\left(p-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v)-a(v).

Since
\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}
is the Lee-Low-Pines transform of the total momentum operator
\mathbb{P}
, we can therefore express
E_{\alpha}(p)
as the ground state energy of the fiber operator
\mathbb{H}_{p}
, i.e.

\displaystyle E_{\alpha}(p)=\inf\sigma(\mathbb{H}_{p}).

In order to establish the upper bound in Eq. (
1.13
), we first note that by the variational characterization of the ground state energy,
E_{\alpha}(p)
is bounded from above by

\displaystyle E_{\alpha}(p)\leq\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}},

for any element
\Psi_{\alpha,p}\in\mathcal{F}
with
\|\Psi_{\alpha,p}\|=1
. It is therefore enough to find a good trial state
\Psi_{\alpha,p}
, such that

(1.15)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\leq E_{\alpha}+\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}+C\alpha^{-(4+\epsilon)}|p|^{2}.

Naively, the most natural candidate would be
\Psi_{\alpha,p}:=\Psi_{\alpha}
, where
\Psi_{\alpha}
is the ground state of the operator
\mathbb{H}_{0}
, i.e.
\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathbb{H}_{0}\Psi_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=E_{\alpha}
and
\|\Psi_{\alpha}\|=1
. In this case however

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=E_{\alpha}+|p|^{2}

would not have the right quartic scaling in
\alpha
, and would only yield a lower bound of the form
m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\alpha)\geq\frac{1}{2}
, i.e. we would not even observe the divergence of the effective mass. The problem is of course, that the term
\left(p-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}
in Eq. (
1.14
) contains a
p
without the right
\alpha
scaling. In order to obtain an improved trial state
\Psi_{\alpha,p}
, let
\mathbb{B}=(\mathbb{B}_{1},\mathbb{B}_{2},\mathbb{B}_{3})
be a (pseudo) boost for
\mathcal{P}
, i.e. a collection of commuting self-adjoint operators
\mathbb{B}_{j}
acting on
\mathcal{F}
that satisfies for
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

(1.16)

\displaystyle e^{-ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\mathcal{P}e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\approx\mathcal{P}+p,

or equivalently for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

(1.17)

\displaystyle e^{iz\cdot\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{B}e^{-iz\cdot\mathcal{P}}\approx\mathbb{B}+z,

and define
\Psi_{\alpha,p}:=e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\Psi_{\alpha}
. We write
\approx
, respectively pseudo-boost, as it is not possible to satisfy Eq. (
1.16
) and Eq. (
1.17
) as an identity. With this choice we have a cancellation of the unwanted
p
-term

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\left(p-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\approx\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathcal{P}^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}.

For the purpose of finding the correct boost
\mathbb{B}
that yields the desired upper bound in Eq. (
1.15
), let us formally apply the semi-classical correspondence principle, which tells us that the energy of the quantum system corresponds to the Peka functional
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}
defined in Eq. (
1.5
), the ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
corresponds to a minimizer
\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}
, the ground state energy
E_{\alpha}
corresponds to the minimum
e^{\mathrm{Pek}}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}
and the unitary transformation
e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}
corresponds formally to a Hamiltonian flow
p\mapsto\varphi_{p}\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
with
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and values in the infinite dimensional space
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)

(1.18)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\alpha^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p}\varphi_{p}=i\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}B\!\left(\varphi(p)\right),\\
\varphi_{0}=\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}},\end{cases}

for a suitable function
B:L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
corresponding to the operator
{\mathbb{B}}
. Since all of these considerations are of purely formal nature, we are not going to worry about the notion of differentiability. It is however worth pointing out that Eq. (
1.17
) corresponds to

(1.19)

\displaystyle B(\varphi_{z})=B(\varphi)+z

for
\varphi\neq 0
. A natural candidate for a function
B
is the mean-value (we ignore at this point that this is ill-defined for some
\varphi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
) with respect to the measure
\|\varphi\|^{-2}|\varphi(y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y
, i.e.

(1.20)

\displaystyle B(\varphi):=\|\varphi\|^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!y\,|\varphi(y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y.

This choice of
B
clearly satisfies Eq. (
1.19
), and the solution of Eq. (
1.18
) reads

\displaystyle\varphi_{p}(y)=e^{i\alpha^{-2}\|\varphi\|^{-2}p\cdot y}\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}(y).

However, since
\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}B\!\left(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right)=\|\varphi\|^{-2}y\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}(y)
is not an
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
function,
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi_{p})
does not exhibit the right quadratic scaling in
p
. To be precise, one can show that there exists a constant
C>0
such that for
p
small enough

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi_{p})\geq e^{\mathrm{Pek}}+C\alpha^{-2}|p|.

In order to make sure that
\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}B\!\left(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right)\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
, let us take the mean-value only in a bounded region. For this purpose let
x^{\lambda}
denote the
\lambda
-quantile of a measure on
\mathbb{R}
, let
\rho_{j}
be the
j
-th marginal distribution of a measure
\rho
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and define
\mathrm{d}\rho_{\varphi}:=|\varphi(y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y
. Then we introduce for
q>0
in components

(1.21)

\displaystyle m_{q}(\rho)_{j}:

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{x^{\frac{1}{2}-q}(\rho_{j})}^{x^{\frac{1}{2}+q}(\rho_{j})}\mathrm{d}\rho_{j}\right)^{-1}\int_{x^{\frac{1}{2}-q}(\rho_{j})}^{x^{\frac{1}{2}+q}(\rho_{j})}y\mathrm{d}\rho_{j}(y),

(1.22)

\displaystyle B(\varphi):

\displaystyle=m_{q}(\chi*\rho_{\varphi}),

where
\chi\geq 0
is a smooth function, see also Section
3
for a comprehensive definition of
m_{q}
. The statistical quantity
m_{q}
has been used previously in the study of translation-invariant Bose gases
[
3
]
as well as in the study of the Fröhlich polaron
[
4
,
5
]
, where it was referred to as the regularized median. Clearly the choice of
B
in Eq. (
1.22
) satisfies Eq. (
1.19
) and since

\displaystyle\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}B\!\left(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right)\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right),

we have the right scaling

(1.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi_{p})=e^{\mathrm{Pek}}+C\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}+o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right),

for a suitable constant
C>0
, where we write
o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right)
for a term that is small compared to
\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}
as
p\rightarrow 0
and
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
.

Finally, we want to modify
B
, such that the corresponding constant
C
in Eq. (
1.23
) is the optimal one
C=\frac{1}{2m_{\mathrm{Pek}}}
. We note that the Landau-Pekar constant
m_{\mathrm{Pek}}
emerges naturally as

(1.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}\!\left(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}-i\frac{p}{\alpha^{2}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}\cdot\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right)=e^{\mathrm{Pek}}+\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}+o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right),

see for example
[
8
]
, where the effective mass is identified for the classical polaron described by the Landau-Pekar equations. Motivated by Eq. (
1.24
), we want to find a function
B
satisfying Eq. (
1.19
) and

(1.25)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}B\!\left(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right)=-\frac{1}{m_{\mathrm{LP}}}\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}.

In the following let us define for
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
the modified boost
B
as

(1.26)

\displaystyle B(\varphi):=m_{q}(\chi*\rho_{\varphi})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f\Big(y-m_{q}(\chi*\rho_{\varphi})\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho_{\varphi}(y),

which satisfies Eq. (
1.19
). An elementary computation shows that

(1.27)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}B\!\left(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right)=\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}m_{q}(\chi*\rho_{\varphi})|_{\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}}+f\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}

in case
f\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\perp\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
, i.e. we can write any vector in the affine space

(1.28)

\displaystyle\xi\in\mathcal{A}:=\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}m_{q}(\chi*\rho_{\varphi})|_{\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}}+\{\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\}^{\perp}

as a gradient
\xi=\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}B\!\left(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right)
of a boost
B
with the right choice of
f
. Notably,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{m_{\mathrm{LP}}}\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\in\mathcal{A}

is an element of
\mathcal{A}
, and therefore we can find a boost
B
such that Eq. (
1.25
) holds. Hence

(1.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi_{p})=e^{\mathrm{Pek}}+\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}+o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right).

Having the correct semi-classical boost
B
at hand such that Eq. (
1.29
) holds, we are going to construct the corresponding boost operator
{\mathbb{B}}
and define the trial state

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha,p}:=e^{ip\cdot{\mathbb{B}}}\Psi_{\alpha}.

There are two main technical challenges in this manuscript. The first one is to establish a rigorous correspondence between the quantum objects
\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p},{\mathbb{B}},\dots
and their semi-classical counterparts
\varphi_{p},\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}},B,\dots
, i.e. we have to verify that

(1.30)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}-E_{\alpha}=\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi_{p})-e^{\mathrm{Pek}}+o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right)=\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}+o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right).

In
[
4
]
, it has been shown that suitable low energy states satisfy Bose-Einstein condensation, which loosely speaking means that a typical phonon configuration is expected to be close to the semi-classical optimizer
\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
. In order to derive Eq. (
1.30
), we are going to improve this result, by showing that the empirical distribution
\rho_{Y_{n}}
of a collection of phonons at position
Y_{n}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
is expected to be close to the Pekar measure
\mathrm{d}\rho^{\mathrm{Pek}}:=|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}|^{2}\mathrm{d}y
with respect to the distance

(1.31)

\displaystyle\|\chi*(\rho_{Y_{n}}-\rho^{\mathrm{Pek}})\|_{\mathrm{TV}},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
refers to the total variation and
\chi
is a mollifier. Furthermore, we show that the notion of distance in Eq. (
1.31
) is the correct one to discuss continuity and differentiability aspects of the statistical quantity
m_{q}(\chi*\rho_{\varphi})
used in the definition of
B
in Eq. (
1.26
). The second technical difficulty concerns the Taylor expansion of the unitary group
e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}
, which is necessary to express the left hand side of Eq. (
1.30
). To be precise, we need to show that the moments of the boost operator
{\mathbb{B}}
are bounded in the ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
, i.e. we have to show that there exist constants
C_{m}
such that for all
\alpha
large enough

(1.32)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},|{\mathbb{B}}|^{m}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle\leq C_{m}.

For this purpose, we use a layer-cake representation of
\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},|{\mathbb{B}}|^{m}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle
and show that the ground state has to be supported mostly on the spectral subspace where
|{\mathbb{B}}|
is small. Related to the question of whether
\nabla_{\overline{\varphi}}B\!\left(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right)
is an element of
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
, we want to emphasise that Eq. (
1.32
) certainly does not hold for any boost
{\mathbb{B}}
. For example the mean-value boost in Eq. (
1.20
) has already on the semi-classical level unbounded second moments

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|y|^{2}\mathrm{d}\rho^{\mathrm{Pek}}=\|y\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}=\infty.

Outline.
The paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
we introduce Fock space formalism, including all the objects appearing in the definition of
\mathbb{H}
, as well as the most important semi-classical objects. In the subsequent Section
3
we will introduce an appropriate boost operator as a counterpart to the semi-classical function
B
defined in Eq. (
1.26
) and give a precise definition of the trial state
\Psi_{\alpha,p}
. Furthermore, we will isolate the main contribution in the energy
\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}
from various error terms
\mathcal{E}
. The proof of Theorem
1.1
is then content of Section
4
, where we confirm the formal semi-classical predictions. In the rest of the paper we will derive technical results necessary for the proof of Theorem
1.1
, starting with the analysis of the regularized median
m_{q}
, see Eq. (
1.21
), in Section
5
. In the following Section
6
we will discuss the spatial distribution of the ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
and establish that
\Psi_{\alpha}
is mostly supported on the spectral subspace where the boost
|{\mathbb{B}}|
is small. In a similar spirit, we verify in Section
7
that the ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
is mostly supported on polarization fields
\varphi
close to the semi-classical minimizer
\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
. The analysis of the error terms
\mathcal{E}
from Section
3
is then content of Section
8
and in Section
9
we will derive improved results on the issue of Bose-Einstein condensation. Especially, we will verify that the empirical measures
\rho_{Y_{n}}
are close to the semi-classical distribution
\rho^{\mathrm{Pek}}
with respect to the regularized total variation. In the Appendix
A
we are going to verify various decay properties of the semi-classical minimizers, in momentum space as well as position space.

2.
Definitions and Fock Space Formalism

2.1.
Basic Notation

It is the content of this Subsection, to introduce the Fock space
\mathcal{F}
, as well as two important families of operators defined on
\mathcal{F}
. Furthermore, we are presenting a collection of useful cut-off functions.

In order to define
\mathcal{F}
, let
\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}
be the (disjoint) union of the topological spaces
\mathbb{R}^{3n}
, and let us denote with
L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
the space of all Borel measurable complex valued (equivalence classes of) functions

\displaystyle\Psi:\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},

such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}\left|\Psi(Y_{n})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}Y_{n}<\infty
, equipped with the inner product

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Psi,\Phi\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}\overline{\Psi(Y_{n})}\Phi(Y_{n})\mathrm{d}Y_{n}.

For the readers convenience, we will indicate the dimension of an element
Y_{n}\in\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}
in our notation, i.e.
Y_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}
, and we will usually structure the vector
Y_{n}
as

\displaystyle Y_{n}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),

with
y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Clearly, the Hilbert space
L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
is isomorphic to the orthogonal sum

\displaystyle L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)\cong\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigoplus}L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right).

We then define the Fock space
\mathcal{F}
as the subspace of
L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
containing all permutation symmetric functions
\Psi
, i.e.
\Psi\in\mathcal{F}
if and only if for all
Y_{n}\in\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}
and permutations
\sigma\in S_{n}

\displaystyle\Psi(y_{\sigma_{1}},\dots,y_{\sigma_{n}})=\Psi(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}).

In case we want to emphasise that the functions involved in an inner product are permutation symmetric, we will write
\Big\langle\Psi,\Phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}
instead of
\Big\langle\Psi,\Phi\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}
. In the following it will be useful to introduce for
\alpha>0
and
Y_{n}\in\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}
the empirical measure

(2.1)

\displaystyle\rho_{Y_{n}}=\alpha^{-2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{y_{j}},

where we suppress the
\alpha
-dependence of
\rho_{Y_{n}}
in our notation for the sake of readability and use the convention that
\rho_{Y_{0}}=0
. Then
\Psi\in L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
is an element of
\mathcal{F}
, if and only if it can be written as a function of the empirical measures, i.e. if and only if there exists a complex valued function
\Psi^{\prime}
taking measures on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as an argument, such that

\displaystyle\Psi(Y_{n})=\Psi^{\prime}(\rho_{Y_{n}}).

For a measurable subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
, let us furthermore introduce the Fock space
\mathcal{F}(\Omega)
as the space of all
\Psi\in\mathcal{F}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}(\Psi)\subseteq\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\Omega^{n}.

The first important family of operators on the spaces
L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
and
\mathcal{F}
are multiplication operators by a given measurable function
F:\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. In the following we will use the notation
F(Y_{n})
for the multiplication operator by
F
, e.g. we will write

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Psi,F(Y_{n})\Psi\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}F(Y_{n})\left|\Psi(Y_{n})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}Y_{n}.

Many of the functions considered in this manuscript will naturally depend only on the empirical measure
\rho_{Y_{n}}
, i.e. will be of the form
F(Y_{n})=f(\rho_{Y_{n}})
, in which case we might write

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Psi,f(\rho_{Y_{n}})\Psi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\Big\langle\Psi,f(\rho_{Y_{n}})\Psi\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}f(\rho_{Y_{n}})\left|\Psi(Y_{n})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}Y_{n}.

An especially important example of a multiplication operator is the (re-scaled) particle number operator
\mathcal{N}
defined as

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}:=\mathcal{N}(\rho_{Y_{n}}):=\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}=\frac{n}{\alpha^{2}}.

The second essential family of operators on a Fock space are the creation and annihilation operators
a^{*}(f)
and
a(f)
, parameterized by functions
f\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
. In order to properly introduce
a^{*}(f)
, let us first define the operator
L(f)
acting on
L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
as

(2.3)

\displaystyle\Big(L(f)\Psi\Big)(Y_{n}):=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha}\Psi(Y_{n-1})f(y_{n}).

Here we have used the convention that
Y_{n}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
and
Y_{n-1}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})
, a notion which we will use repeatedly in this article. We can now define on
\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle a^{*}(f):

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{F}}L(f)\iota_{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle a(f):

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{F}}L(f)^{*}\iota_{\mathcal{F}},

where
A^{*}
is the adjoint of an operator
A
,
\iota_{\mathcal{F}}
is the embedding of
\mathcal{F}\subseteq L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
and
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}=\iota_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{F}
.

The operators
a^{*}(f)
and
a(f)
will be a convenient tool to express many other operators acting on
\mathcal{F}
, e.g. for a bounded and measurable function
h:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and an orthonormal basis
\{\varphi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
we can express the multiplication operator
Y_{n}\mapsto\int h\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}
as

(2.4)

\displaystyle\int h\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\braket{\varphi_{m},h\varphi_{n}}a^{*}(\varphi_{m})a(\varphi_{n}).

Therefore, we see that the definition of the particle number operator in Eq. (
2.2
) and the definition given previously in Eq. (
1.3
) coincide.

Before giving a proper introduction of the Fröhlich Hamiltonian in the following Subsection
2.2
, we shall introduce some useful notation. Following
[
4
,
5
]
, we are going to introduce for some
\kappa\geq 0
and
-\infty\leq a<b\leq\infty
, and a function
f:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined on some set
U
, cut-off functions

\displaystyle\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq f\leq b):\begin{cases}U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\\
t\mapsto\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq f(t)\leq b),\end{cases}

where
\kappa\geq 0
is going to determine the sharpness of the cut-off. Usually we will take
U
as the set
\mathbb{R}
,
\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}
or the set of all Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In order to introduce
\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq f\leq b)
, let

\displaystyle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}:\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}\longrightarrow[0,1]

be smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\beta_{1}^{2}+\beta_{2}^{2}=1
,
\mathrm{supp}\!\left(\beta_{1}\right)\subseteq[-\infty,1)
and
\mathrm{supp}\!\left(\beta_{2}\right)\subseteq(-1,\infty]
. Then we define
\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq f\leq b)
as

(2.5)

\displaystyle\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq f(t)\leq b):=\begin{cases}\beta_{1}\!\left(\frac{f(t)-b}{\kappa}\right)\beta_{2}\!\left(\frac{f(t)-a}{\kappa}\right),\ \mathrm{for}\ \kappa>0\\
\mathds{1}_{[a,b]}(f(t)),\ \mathrm{for}\ \kappa=0.\end{cases}

Clearly,

\displaystyle\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq f(t)\leq b)

\displaystyle=1\ \ \mbox{in case}\ \ f(t)\in[a+\kappa,b-\kappa],

\displaystyle\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq f(t)\leq b)

\displaystyle=0\ \ \mbox{in case}\ \ f(t)\notin[a-\kappa,b+\kappa].

Furthermore, we define for a self-adjoint operator
T
the contraction

\displaystyle\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq T\leq b)

by the means of spectral calculus, and we write
\chi(a\leq f\leq b)
in case
\kappa=0
and
\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq f)
, respectively
\chi_{\kappa}(f\leq b)
, in case
b=\infty
, respectively
a=-\infty
. Note that for
\kappa>0

\displaystyle t\mapsto\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq t\leq b)

is a smooth function and in case the additional condition
a+\kappa<b-\kappa
is satisfied we have that

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\chi_{\kappa}(a\leq t\leq b)^{2}}=\sqrt{\chi_{\kappa}(t\leq a)^{2}+\chi_{\kappa}(b\leq t)^{2}}=\chi_{\kappa}(t\leq a)+\chi_{\kappa}(b\leq t)

is a smooth function as well.

2.2.
The Fröhlich Hamiltonian

With the definitions of the previous Subsection
2.1
at hand, we are in a position to properly introduce the polaron model. The Hilbert space under consideration is given by the tensor product

\displaystyle L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}\subseteq L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right),

which we can naturally identify as a subspace of
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
. Here the first factor
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the space of the electron coordinate
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}
contains the coordinates
Y_{n}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
of the elementary excitations of the polarization field. For a function
\Psi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)
, we will usually write
\Psi(x;Y_{n})
and
\nabla_{x}
as well as
\Delta_{x}
for the gradient, respectively the Laplace operator, in the direction of
x
. Similarly, we define

(2.6)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}\Psi)(x;Y_{n}):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{y_{j}}\Psi(x;Y_{n}).

Note that with these notations at hand, the total momentum operator
\mathbb{P}
reads

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}=\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}+\mathcal{P}.

With
\Delta_{x},\mathcal{N}
,
a^{*}(f)
and
a(f)
, we have all objects at hand in order to introduce

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}:=-\Delta_{x}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v_{x})-a(v_{x}),

see Eq. (
1.2
). However, since
v_{x}\notin L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
, due to an ultraviolet singularity, the definition in Eq. (
1.2
) is only formal. In order to properly define
\mathbb{H}
, let us first construct a quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}
, acting on the space of permutation symmetric compactly supported
C^{\infty}
functions as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\Psi):=\left\langle\Psi,\left(-\Delta_{x}+\mathcal{N}\right)\Psi\right\rangle-2\mathfrak{Re}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}v(y_{n}-x)\overline{\Psi(x;Y_{n})}\Psi(x;Y_{n-1})\mathrm{d}Y_{n}\mathrm{d}x.

Since
\Psi
is compactly supported, there exists a
n_{*}
such that
\Psi(Y_{n})=0
for
n\geq n_{*}
and
(x;Y_{n})\mapsto\overline{\Psi(x;Y_{n})}\Psi(x;Y_{n-1})
is a bounded function with compact support for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Together with the fact that
v
is locally
L^{1}
, we observe that
\mathcal{Q}(\Psi)
is well defined. Furthermore, it follows from
[
18
]
, that the quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}
is bounded from below and closable, and therefore defines a self-adjoint operator
\mathbb{H}
. Similarly, we can give meaning to the formal definitions

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v)-a(v),

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{p}

\displaystyle:=\left(p-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v)-a(v).

It is easy to see that
\mathbb{H}
is unitary equivalent to
\mathbb{H}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}
by centering the coordinate frame of the polarization field at the position of the electron
x
, i.e. we define the unitary map

(2.7)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}\Psi)(x;Y_{n}):=\Psi(x;y_{1}-x,\dots,y_{n}-x)

and observe that
\mathbb{H}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}=\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{H}\mathcal{T}
. In this manuscript, we will use both representations
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{H}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}
, where we prefer
\mathbb{H}
in case we want to establish a semi-classical correspondence and use
\mathbb{H}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}
in case we want to work with a proper ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
of the fiber Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}_{0}
.

Finally, let us introduce the modified versions
\mathbb{H}^{K}
and
\mathbb{H}^{K}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}
, which include an ultraviolet regularization. For this purpose let us use for
K>0
the projection
\chi(|\nabla|\leq K)
, see Eq. (
2.5
), in order to define the regularized interaction

\displaystyle v^{K}:=\chi(|\nabla|\leq K)v.

With
v^{K}
at hand we define the operators

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{K}:

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{x}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v^{K}_{x})-a(v^{K}_{x}),

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{K}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}:

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v^{K})-a(v^{K}),

which satisfy
\mathbb{H}^{K}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla_{x}}=\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{H}^{K}\mathcal{T}
.

2.3.
Semi-Classical Objects

In this Subsection we want to introduce the most important objects appearing in the semi-classical correspondence principle. For this purpose let us introduce the vacuum state
\Xi\in\mathcal{F}
as
\Xi(Y_{n}):=0
for
n\geq 1
and
\Xi(Y_{0})=1
for
Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{0}=\{0\}
, and let us define for
f\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
the unitary Weyl transformation
W_{f}
acting on
\mathcal{F}
as

(2.10)

\displaystyle W_{f}:=e^{\alpha^{2}a(f)-\alpha^{2}a^{*}(f)}.

Note that, up to a phase, the Weyl transformations
W_{f}
are characterized by the law

\displaystyle W_{g}^{*}a(f)W_{g}=a(f)-\braket{f,g}.

With
\Xi
and
W_{f}
at hand, we can introduce for
\psi,\varphi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
, where
\|\psi\|=1
, a family of semi-classical trial states

(2.11)

\displaystyle\psi\otimes\Xi_{\varphi}:=\psi\otimes\left(W_{\varphi}^{*}\Xi\right)\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}.

Notably, the coherent states
\Xi_{\varphi}
are joint eigenfunctions of the annihilation operators
a(f)
, i.e.

(2.12)

\displaystyle a(f)\Xi_{\varphi}=\braket{f,\varphi}\Xi_{\varphi},

a property which characterizes them up to a constant. Together with Eq. (
2.4
), we observe the following correspondence between the measure
\mathrm{d}\rho(y):=|\varphi(y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y
, a semi-classical object, and the empirical measure

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Xi_{\varphi},\int h\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}\Xi_{\varphi}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\int h\mathrm{d}\rho_{\varphi}.

Furthermore, by Eq. (
2.12
) and Eq. (
2.13
), we obtain that the energy of
\psi\otimes\Xi_{\varphi}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\psi,\varphi):=\left\langle\psi\otimes\Xi_{\varphi},\mathbb{H}\psi\otimes\Xi_{\varphi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}}=\|\varphi\|^{2}+\left\langle\psi,\left(-\Delta_{x}-2v*\mathfrak{Re}[\varphi]\right)\psi\right\rangle.

Both
\mathbb{H}
as well as
\Xi_{\varphi}=W_{\varphi}\Xi
depend on the parameter
\alpha>0
, since the definition of
a^{*}(f)
and
a(f)
, respectively
L(f)
in Eq. (
2.3
), is
\alpha
dependent, however the energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\psi,\varphi)=\|\varphi\|^{2}+\left\langle\psi,\left(-\Delta_{x}-2v*\mathfrak{Re}[\varphi]\right)\psi\right\rangle,

is
\alpha
independent. Minimizing over all possible electron functions
\psi
yields the semi-classical Pekar functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi):=\inf_{\psi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right):\|\psi\|=1}\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\psi,\varphi)=\|\varphi\|^{2}+\inf\sigma\!\left(-\Delta_{x}-2v*\mathfrak{Re}[\varphi]\right).

According to
[
13
]
, the functional
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Pek}}
has unique minimizers
(\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}},\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}})
such that
\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
is non-negative and
\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
is radial, and all other minimizers are of the form
(e^{i\theta}\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}_{z},\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}_{z})
for
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Consequently,
\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
is a minimizer of
\mathcal{F}
as well, and we define the minimal Pekar energy
e^{\mathrm{Pek}}
as

(2.14)

\displaystyle e^{\mathrm{Pek}}:=\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}})=\inf_{\varphi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)}\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\varphi)=\inf_{\varphi,\psi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right):\|\psi\|=1}\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Pek}}(\psi,\varphi).

Let us furthermore denote with
P^{\mathrm{Pek}}:L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\longrightarrow L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
the orthogonal projection onto
\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
and with
Q^{\mathrm{Pek}}:L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\longrightarrow L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
the orthogonal projection onto
\{\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\}^{\perp}
, i.e. we define

\displaystyle(P^{\mathrm{Pek}}\psi)(x):

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\overline{\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}(x^{\prime})}\psi(x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}(x),

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{Pek}}:

\displaystyle=1-P^{\mathrm{Pek}},

and let us define the Pekar measure
\rho^{\mathrm{Pek}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as
\mathrm{d}\rho^{\mathrm{Pek}}(y):=\left|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}(y)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}y
.

The following Lemma
2.1
provides useful identities and estimates for the minimizers
(\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}},\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}})
of
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Pek}}
and the truncated interaction terms

(2.15)

\displaystyle v^{\Lambda}:

\displaystyle=\chi(|\nabla|\leq\Lambda)v,

(2.16)

\displaystyle w^{\Lambda}:

\displaystyle=\frac{i\nabla}{\Delta}\chi(|\nabla|>\Lambda)v.

The proof of Lemma
2.1
will be the content of Appendix
A
.

Lemma 2.1
.

We have the identities
|\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}|^{2}*v=\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}
and

(2.17)

\displaystyle v=v^{\Lambda}+\frac{1}{i}\nabla\cdot w^{\Lambda}.

Furthermore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\Lambda>0
and
R>0

(2.18)

\displaystyle\left\|v^{\Lambda}\right\|\leq C\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}},\ \ \ \ \left\|w^{\Lambda}\right\|\leq C\Lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(2.19)

\displaystyle\ \ \left\|\chi\!\left(|\nabla|>\Lambda\right)\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right\|\leq C\Lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(2.20)

\displaystyle\ \ \left\|\chi\!\left(|y|>R\right)\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\right\|\leq CR^{-\frac{1}{2}},

and
\frac{\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}}{\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}}\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

2.4.
Conventions and Definitions

In this Subsection we want to state some conventions and definitions that will be used repeatedly throughout this article.

Convention.
Most importantly,
\alpha
will always refer to a parameter
\alpha\geq 1
, which appears for example in the definition of the operator
L(f)
in Eq. (
2.3
), and therefore in the definition of the creation and annihilation operators
a(f)
and
a^{*}(f)
, and in the definition of the Fröhlich Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}
. Furthermore, the empirical measures
\rho_{Y_{n}}
defined in Eq. (
2.1
) depends on
\alpha
, and therefore all functions of
\rho_{Y_{n}}
do so as well. For the sake of readability, we will not always indicate the dependence on
\alpha
. Furthermore, if not indicated otherwise, all estimates are supposed to hold uniformly in
\alpha
as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
, e.g. we write

\displaystyle X\lesssim Y,

for
\alpha
-dependent objects
X
and
Y
, in case there exists an
\alpha_{0}\geq 1
and a
C>0
, such that

\displaystyle X\leq CY,

for all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
. Similarly,
p
will always refer to an elements
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and, if not indicated otherwise, estimates are supposed to hold uniform in
p
.

The second convention we will make use of, is that multiplication operators by functions
Y_{n}\mapsto F(Y_{n})
will be denoted simply by
F(Y_{n})
, and in case
F
is in a natural way a function
f
of the empirical measure we write
f(\rho_{Y_{n}})
. Occasionally we will further write
F(y_{n})
for multiplication operators depending only on the component
y_{n}
in
Y_{n}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
.

Finally, we call an element
\Psi\in\mathcal{H}
of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
a state, in case
\|\Psi\|=1
.

Definition.
Besides the notation
\chi(a\leq f\leq b)
introduced in Eq. (
2.5
), we will denote with
g
a (fixed) rotationally symmetric mollifier on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, i.e.
g:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow[0,1]
will denote a smooth function with compact support,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}g(x)\mathrm{d}x=1
and
g(x)=g(y)
in case
|x|=|y|
. Furthermore, it will be useful to have mollifiers at hand that are convolutions them self, and therefore we introduce the function

\displaystyle\chi:=g*g,

which is clearly a rotationally symmetric mollifier again. It will also be convenient to have for
T>0
the re-scaled (rotationally symmetric) molllifiers
g_{T}
at hand

\displaystyle g_{T}(y):=T^{3}g(Ty).

We will furthermore use a third family of smooth cut-off functions
\tau_{\eta}
defined as

\displaystyle\tau_{\eta}(y):=\eta^{-\frac{3}{2}}\tau\!\left(\eta^{-1}y\right),

where
\tau:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow[0,1]
is smooth with support in
\left\{y:|y|\leq\frac{1}{3}\right\}
and satisfies
\int\tau(y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y=1
.

In addition let us fix some constants, used throughout this paper. In the following let
\delta_{*}
be such that
0<\delta_{*}<\frac{\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{4}}{2}
, and pick
\kappa,\sigma
and
q
, such that
0<\kappa<\sigma
and

(2.21)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \sigma+\kappa<\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2},

\displaystyle\delta_{*}+\sigma+\kappa<\frac{(\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}-\sigma-\kappa)^{2}}{2},

\displaystyle\ 0<q<\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\delta_{*}+\sigma+\kappa}{(\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}-\sigma-\kappa)^{2}}.

Moreover, since
\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
,
\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}>0
and
\delta_{*}<\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{4}
, we find a
R_{*}>0
such that

\displaystyle\underset{|x-y|>R_{*}}{\int\int}|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}(x)|^{2}|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}(y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\delta_{*}.

With
\delta_{*}
and
R_{*}
at hand, let us introduce for
\lambda>0

(2.22)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\lambda}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Omega_{\lambda}^{(n)}

as the set of all
Y_{n}\in\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}
, such that

\displaystyle\ \ \|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}-\lambda\leq\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}\leq\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}+\lambda,

\displaystyle\delta_{*}-\lambda\leq\underset{|x-y|>R_{*}}{\int\int}\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}(x)\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}(y)\leq\delta_{*}+\lambda.

It will be the main result of Section
7
that the ground state concentrates on sets of the form
\Omega_{\lambda}
for
\lambda>0
, as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
. Furthermore, we demonstrate in Section
5
that
\sigma
and
\kappa
are chosen in such a way, that the regularized median
m_{q}
introduced in Eq. (
1.21
) satisfies uniform continuity and differentiability properties on
\Omega_{\sigma+\kappa}
, see also the assumptions of
[
4
, Lemma 3.10]
.

3.
Construction of a Trial State

It is the content of this Section, to construct a trial state
\Psi_{\alpha,p}\in\mathcal{F}
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\leq E_{\alpha}+\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}+C\alpha^{-(4+\epsilon)}|p|^{2},

as is shown in the subsequent Section
4
. Following the strategy proposed in Section
1
, right after Theorem
1.1
, we will define
\Psi_{\alpha,p}
as the ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
of the fiber Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}_{0}
boosted by the unitary group
e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}
. Applying the (pseudo) boost
e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}
is a necessary procedure, as we want to have a cancellation of the
p
-term appearing in the fiber Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{p}=\left(p-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{N}-a^{*}(v)-a(v).

Choosing the right (pseudo) boost, i.e. choosing the right self-adjoint operators
\mathbb{B}=(\mathbb{B}_{1},\mathbb{B}_{2},\mathbb{B}_{3})
satisfying
e^{-ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\mathcal{P}e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\approx\mathcal{P}+p
, is a delicate task, which we are going to elaborate on in the following Subsection
3.1
. The state
\Psi_{\alpha,p}
is then introduced in Eq. (
3.11
).

3.1.
The correct Way to Boost

On a semi-classical level, the right boost is generated by the function
B
, in the sense of Eq. (
1.18
), defined on the phase space
L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
as

(3.1)

\displaystyle B(\varphi):=m_{q}(\chi*\rho_{\varphi})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f\Big(y-m_{q}(\chi*\rho_{\varphi})\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho_{\varphi}(y)

for a suitable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, see Eq. (
1.26
) and the discussion below, which we assume to be in
C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
. It will be the goal of this Subsection to find a quantum counterpart
{\mathbb{B}}=({\mathbb{B}}_{1},{\mathbb{B}}_{2},{\mathbb{B}}_{3})
to
B
in the form of self-adjoint commuting operators
{\mathbb{B}}_{j}
defined on
\mathcal{F}
.

In advance we are giving a proper definition of the regularized median
m_{q}
appearing in the definition of
B
in Eq. (
3.1
), see also
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
for previous appearances of the statistical quantity
m_{q}
. Following
[
4
]
, we define for
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
the
\lambda
-quantile of a (finite, Borel) measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
as

(3.2)

\displaystyle x^{\lambda}(\nu):=\sup\!\left\{t:\int_{-\infty}^{t}\mathrm{d}\nu\leq\lambda\int\mathrm{d}\nu\right\}.

Furthermore, let
\rho_{j}
denote for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
the
j
-th marginal distribution of a (finite, Borel) measure
\rho
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, defined by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}h\mathrm{d}\rho_{j}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h(y_{j})\mathrm{d}\rho(y).

With these notations at hand, and
K_{q}(\nu):=\left[x^{\frac{1}{2}-q}(\nu),x^{\frac{1}{2}+q}(\nu)\right]\subseteq\mathbb{R}
for
q>0
, we define the regularized median
m_{q}(\rho)=(m_{q}(\rho)_{1},m_{q}(\rho)_{2},m_{q}(\rho)_{3})
in coordinates as

\displaystyle m_{q}(\rho)_{j}:=\left(\int\mathds{1}_{K_{q}(\rho_{j})}\mathrm{d}\rho_{j}\right)^{-1}\!\int\mathds{1}_{K_{q}(\rho_{j})}(t)\,t\mathrm{d}\rho_{j}(t),

with the convention that
m_{q}(0):=0
. Finally, recall for
\varphi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
the definition of the measure
\rho_{\varphi}
from Section
1

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\rho_{\varphi}(y):=|\varphi(y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y.

In order to find a quantum counterpart to the semi-classical object
B
from Eq. (
3.1
), observe the formal correspondence between the empirical measure
\rho_{Y_{n}}
defined in Eq. (
2.1
) and the measure
\rho_{\varphi}
defined in Eq. (
3.4
), see for example Eq. (
2.13
). Therefore, a natural candidate for the boost
\mathbb{B}
would be given by the multiplication operator
\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})
with

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\rho):=m_{q}(\chi*\rho)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f\Big(y-m_{q}(\chi*\rho)\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho(y).

Since we will require a multiplication operator that satisfies certain continuity and differentiability properties, we will use a slightly modified version
G
instead of the function
\mathcal{B}
from Eq. (
3.5
), defined as

\displaystyle G(\rho):=F(\rho)\mathcal{B}(\rho),

where the function

(3.6)

\displaystyle F(\rho):

\displaystyle=\chi_{\kappa^{2}}\!\left(\left(\int\mathrm{d}\rho-\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\underset{|x-y|>R_{*}}{\int\int}\mathrm{d}\rho\mathrm{d}\rho-\delta_{*}\right)^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}\right)

makes sure that
G
is only supported on regular enough measures
\rho
. The operator
\mathbb{B}
, which we will use as the generator of a unitary group, is then defined as

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}:=G(\rho_{Y_{n}})=F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}}).

The constants
\kappa,R_{*},\delta_{*}
and
\sigma
appearing in the definition of
F
as well as the constant
q
and the mollifier
\chi
appearing in the definition of
\mathcal{B}
are chosen according to Subsection
2.4
.

Before we define the trial state
\Psi_{\alpha,p}
, let us make some observations about the spectrum of the fiber Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}_{0}
and analyse the operators
\mathbb{B}=(\mathbb{B}_{1},\mathbb{B}_{2},\mathbb{B}_{3})
. First of all, it has been shown in
[
7
]
, respectively
[
19
]
, that
E_{\alpha}
is an isolated and non-degenerate point in the spectrum of
\mathbb{H}_{0}
. As an immediate consequence we obtain the following Lemma
3.1
, which establishes the existence of a unique reflection symmetric ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
of
\mathbb{H}_{0}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}:\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}
be defined as
(\mathcal{S}\Psi)(Y_{n}):=\Psi(-Y_{n})
for all
Y_{n}\in\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}
. Then the operator
\mathbb{H}_{0}
has, up to a phase, a unique ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{F}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\Psi_{\alpha}=\Psi_{\alpha}.

Furthermore, the phase can be chosen such that
\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})\geq 0
for all
Y_{n}
.

Proof.

By
[
7
]
, respectively
[
19
]
, we know that
E_{\alpha}
is an isolated and non-degenerate point in the spectrum of
\mathbb{H}_{0}
, i.e. there exists, up to a phase, a unique ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
. Since the unitary operator
\mathcal{S}
commutes with
\mathbb{H}_{0}
, i.e.
\mathcal{S}^{*}\mathbb{H}_{0}\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{H}_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{S}\Psi_{\alpha},\mathbb{H}_{0}\mathcal{S}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathbb{H}_{0}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle=E_{\alpha}.

Consequently,
\mathcal{S}\Psi_{\alpha}
is a ground state as well and by the uniqueness of ground states there exists a
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
with
\mathcal{S}\Psi_{\alpha}=e^{i\theta}\Psi_{\alpha}
. A simple computation exhibits that
|\Psi_{\alpha}|\in\mathcal{F}
defined as
|\Psi_{\alpha}|(Y_{n}):=\left|\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})\right|
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\langle|\Psi_{\alpha}|,\mathbb{H}\,|\Psi_{\alpha}|\right\rangle\leq\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle=E_{\alpha},

i.e.
|\Psi_{\alpha}|
is a ground state again and by the uniqueness of ground states we can therefore assume w.l.o.g. that
\Psi_{\alpha}\geq 0
. Hence,
\theta=0
.
∎

The support of the function
G
in
\mathbb{B}=G(\rho_{Y_{n}})
is clearly contained in the support of
F

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}\!\left(G\right)\subseteq\mathrm{supp}\!\left(F\right),

and using the set
\Omega_{\lambda}
defined in Eq. (
2.22
) we are going to localize the support of
F
in the following Lemma
3.2
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
F
be as in Eq. (
3.6
) and
\Omega_{\lambda}
as in Eq. (
2.22
). Then,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\frac{\sigma-\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}}\subseteq\left\{Y_{n}:F(\rho_{Y_{n}})=1\right\}\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(F)\subseteq\Omega_{\sigma+\kappa}.

Moreover,
\Omega_{\sigma+\kappa}\subseteq\left\{Y_{n}:\rho_{Y_{n}}\neq 0\right\}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\left\{Y_{n}:F(\rho_{Y_{n}})=1\right\}\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(F)=\left\{Y_{n}:F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\neq 0\right\}
is trivial. In order to see that
\mathrm{supp}(F)
is contained in
\Omega_{\sigma+\kappa}
, note that by the definition of the cut-off function
\chi_{\kappa^{2}}(\cdot\leq\sigma^{2})
in Eq. (
2.5
),
F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\neq 0
implies

\displaystyle\left(\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}-\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\underset{|x-y|>R_{*}}{\int\int}\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}-\delta_{*}\right)^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}+\kappa^{2}\leq(\sigma+\kappa)^{2},

and therefore
Y_{n}\in\Omega_{\sigma+\kappa}
, since we have both

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left|\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}-\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}\right|\leq\sigma+\kappa,

\displaystyle\left|\underset{|x-y|>R_{*}}{\int\int}\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}-\delta_{*}\right|\leq\sigma+\kappa.

On the other hand, in case
Y_{n}\in\Omega_{\frac{\sigma-\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}}
, we clearly have

\displaystyle\left(\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}-\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\underset{|x-y|>R_{*}}{\int\int}\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}-\delta_{*}\right)^{2}\leq(\sigma-\kappa)^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}-\kappa^{2},

where we have used
\kappa\leq 1
. Since
\chi_{\kappa^{2}}(x\leq\sigma^{2})=1
for
x\leq\sigma^{2}-\kappa^{2}
, this concludes the proof of Eq. (
3.8
). Finally, recall that
\sigma+\kappa<\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}
, and therefore we have for
Y_{n}\in\Omega_{\sigma+\kappa}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}\geq\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}-\left|\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}-\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}\right|\geq\|\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\|^{2}-(\sigma+\kappa)>0.

∎

In the following we will consider
\Omega_{\sigma+\kappa}
as a set of regular configurations, e.g. we have
\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}>0
for all
Y_{n}\in\mathrm{supp}(F)
by Lemma
3.2
. While it is clear that the components of
\mathbb{B}=(\mathbb{B}_{1},\mathbb{B}_{2},\mathbb{B}_{3})
commute, the following Lemma
3.3
demonstrates that both the multiplication operator
\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})
as well as the operator
\mathbb{B}
are pseudo boosts, see Eq. (
1.16
), where we anticipate that the ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}
is mostly supported on configurations
Y_{n}\in\Omega_{\frac{\sigma-\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}}
, see Section
7
, and hence
\Psi_{\alpha}
is mostly supported on configurations satisfying both
\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}>0
and
F(\rho_{Y_{n}})=1
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be as in Eq. (
3.5
) and
\mathbb{B}
as in Eq. (
3.7
). Then,

(3.9)

\displaystyle e^{-ip\cdot\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\mathcal{P}e^{ip\cdot\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}+\chi\!\left(\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}>0\right)\!p,

(3.10)

\displaystyle e^{-ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\mathcal{P}e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}+F(\rho_{Y_{n}})p.

Proof.

Let us define for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
the shift of a measure
\rho
as

\displaystyle\int h\mathrm{d}\rho_{z}:=\int h(y+z)\mathrm{d}\rho(y),

and compute for
\Psi\in\mathcal{F}
using the definition of
\mathcal{P}
, see Eq. (
2.6
),

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \left(e^{-ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\mathcal{P}e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\Psi-\mathcal{P}\Psi\right)(Y_{n})=\frac{1}{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}e^{-ip\cdot G(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\nabla_{y_{j}}\left(e^{ip\cdot G(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\right)\Psi(Y_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}e^{-ip\cdot G(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\nabla_{z}\Big|_{z=0}\!\left(e^{ip\cdot G\left((\rho_{Y_{n}})_{z}\right)}\right)\!\Psi(Y_{n})\!=\!\frac{1}{i}e^{-ip\cdot G(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\nabla_{z}\Big|_{z=0}\!\left(e^{ip\cdot\mathcal{B}\left((\rho_{Y_{n}})_{z}\right)F\left((\rho_{Y_{n}})_{z}\right)}\right)\!\Psi(Y_{n})

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ =\frac{1}{i}e^{-ip\cdot G(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\nabla_{z}\Big|_{z=0}\left(e^{ip\cdot\left(\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})+z\right)F(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\right)\Psi(Y_{n})=F(\rho_{Y_{n}})p\,\Psi(Y_{n}).

where we have used
\mathcal{B}((\rho_{Y_{n}})_{z})=\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})+z
in case
\int\mathrm{d}\rho_{Y_{n}}>0
and
F(\rho_{Y_{n}})=0
in case
\rho_{Y_{n}}=0
, see Lemma
3.2
. Eq. (
3.9
) can be verified analogously.
∎

Having the (unique, symmetric) ground state
\Psi_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{F}
of
\mathbb{H}_{0}
at hand, see Lemma
3.1
, and the (multiplication) operator
\mathbb{B}
defined in Eq. (
3.7
), we introduce the trial state
\Psi_{\alpha,p}\in\mathcal{F}
as

(3.11)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha,p}:=e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\Psi_{\alpha}=\left(Y_{n}\mapsto e^{ip\cdot G(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})\right)=\left(Y_{n}\mapsto e^{ip\cdot F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})}\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})\right),

which depends on the function
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
in Eq. (
3.5
). Note that we optimize the choice of
f
in Section
4
, however the constants
\kappa,R_{*},\delta_{*},\sigma
and
q
, and the mollifier
\chi
, appearing in the definition of
F
and
\mathcal{B}
, and therefore in the definition of
\mathbb{B}
, should be considered as being fixed according to Subsection
2.4
. It will be the main challenge of this manuscript, to show that the quantum energy

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}-E_{\alpha}\leq\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}+o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right).

is to leading order bounded from above by the semi-classical prediction
\frac{|p|^{2}}{2\alpha^{4}m_{\mathrm{LP}}}
for an optimal choice of
f
.

3.2.
Isolating the essential Contribution in
\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}

In this Subsection we want to compute the energy
\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}
, and isolate the leading order term from contributions that are small compared to
\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}
. In a first step, we provide a rather explicit expression for the energy in the following Lemma
3.4
, using the function
\xi_{p}:\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
defined as

(3.12)

\displaystyle\xi_{p}(Y_{n}):=e^{ip\cdot\left[G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})-G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]}-1-ip\cdot\left[G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})-G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right].

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
f\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
be reflection anti-symmetric, i.e.
f(-y)=-f(y)
, and let
\Psi_{\alpha,p}
be the state defined in Eq. (
3.11
). Then we have the identity

(3.13)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=E_{\alpha}-2\mathfrak{Re}\!\left[\big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +|p|^{2}\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}.

Furthermore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\leq C\alpha^{-5}.

Proof.

By Eq. (
3.10
), we can write

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\left(p\!-\!\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\!=\!\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},e^{-ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\left(p\!-\!\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ =\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left(p\!-\!e^{-ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\mathcal{P}e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\right)^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\Big(\big[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\big]p-\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathcal{P}^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}+|p|^{2}\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}+2p\mathfrak{Re}\!\left[\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]\mathcal{P}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\right].

Note that
\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]\mathcal{P}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=0
, since
\Psi_{\alpha}
is invariant under the reflection
\mathcal{S}
, see Lemma
3.1
, and
\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]\mathcal{P}
is relection anti-symmetric, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{*}\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]\mathcal{P}\mathcal{S}=\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]\mathcal{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{S}=-\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]\mathcal{P}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\left(p\!-\!\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathcal{P}^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}+|p|^{2}\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}.

We observe that both
e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}
and
\mathcal{N}
are multiplication operators and therefore

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathcal{N}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},e^{-ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\mathcal{N}e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathcal{N}e^{-ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}e^{ip\cdot\mathbb{B}}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathcal{N}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}.

Finally, recall the definition of
L(v)
and
a^{*}(v)
from Subsection
2.1
and compute

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},a^{*}(v)\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\!

\displaystyle=\!\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},L(v)\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!\!=\!\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}\!\!\overline{\Psi_{\alpha,p}(Y_{n})}\Psi_{\alpha,p}(Y_{n-1})v(y_{n})\mathrm{d}Y_{n}

(3.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}\!\!e^{ip\cdot\left[G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})-G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]}\overline{\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})}\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n-1})v(y_{n})\mathrm{d}Y_{n}.

Due to our assumption that
f
is reflection anti-symmetric, we obtain that
G(\rho_{-Y_{n}})=-G(\rho_{Y_{n}})
. Using again that
\Psi_{\alpha}
is reflection invariant, therefore yields

(3.16)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}\!\!\left[G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})-G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]\overline{\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})}\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n-1})v(y_{n})\mathrm{d}Y_{n}=0.

Combining Eq. (
3.15
), Eq. (
3.16
) and the definition of
\xi_{p}
in Eq. (
3.12
), we observe that

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},a^{*}(v)\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},a^{*}(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}\xi_{p}(Y_{n})\overline{\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})}\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n-1})v(y_{n})\mathrm{d}Y_{n}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},a^{*}(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}+\Big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}.

This concludes the proof of Eq. (
3.13
), since

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathbb{H}_{p}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\left(p\!-\!\mathcal{P}\right)^{2}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}+\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},\mathcal{N}\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}-2\mathfrak{Re}\!\left[\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha,p},a^{*}(v)\Psi_{\alpha,p}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\right]

\displaystyle\ \ =\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathcal{P}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}+\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\mathcal{N}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}-2\mathfrak{Re}\!\left[\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},a^{*}(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\right]+|p|^{2}\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -2\mathfrak{Re}\!\left[\Big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\ \ =E_{\alpha}-2\mathfrak{Re}\!\left[\Big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\right]+|p|^{2}\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}.

Regarding Eq. (
3.14
), note that
\mathrm{supp}\!\left(1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right)\subseteq\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\setminus\Omega_{\frac{\sigma-\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}}
by Eq. (
3.8
), hence

\displaystyle\left\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\left[1-F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right]^{2}\Psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}\setminus\Omega_{\frac{\sigma-\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}}^{(n)}}\left|\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}Y_{n}=:P_{\alpha}\!\left(\frac{\sigma-\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}\right).

In Lemma
7.5
we are going to show that
P_{\alpha}(\lambda)\leq C_{\lambda}\alpha^{-5}
for
\lambda>0
and a suitable
C_{\lambda}>0
.
∎

In the remainder of this Section, we are going to isolate the main contribution of the term

(3.17)

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}

appearing in Eq. (
3.13
). In order to simplify the analysis later, we are first going to address the ultraviolet singularity of the interaction term
v(y)=\pi^{-\frac{3}{2}}|y|^{-2}
. Using the decomposition

\displaystyle v=v^{\Lambda}+\frac{1}{i}\nabla\cdot w^{\Lambda},

see Eq. (
2.17
), we can split the term in Eq. (
3.17
) according to

(3.18)

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v)\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}=\Big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}+\mathcal{E}_{1},

where
v^{\Lambda}
is an ultraviolet regularized interaction satisfying
\|v^{\Lambda}\|\leq C\sqrt{\Lambda}
, see Eq. (
2.18
), and the residuum
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is defined as

(3.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\frac{1}{i}\Big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(\nabla\cdot w^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}.

It is content of Lemma
8.2
to show that the error term is small compared to
\frac{|p|^{2}}{\sqrt{\Lambda}\alpha^{4}}
, i.e. taking the cut-off parameter
\Lambda
of the order
\alpha^{\epsilon}
for
\epsilon>0
will yield a term small compared to
\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}
. In order to further analyse the first term on the right hand side of Eq. (
3.18
)

(3.20)

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)},

we are going to Taylor expand the exponential in the definition of
\xi_{p}
in Eq. (
3.12
), i.e. we write

(3.21)

\displaystyle\xi_{p}(Y_{n})=-\frac{1}{2}\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})-p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}+\left(\xi_{p}(Y_{n})+\frac{1}{2}\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})-p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\right),

where the expression in
\big(\dots\big)
is the corresponding Taylor residuum. Based on Eq. (
3.21
), we are going to decompose Eq. (
3.20
) according to

(3.22)

\displaystyle\Big\langle\!\Psi_{\alpha},\xi_{p}(Y_{n})L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\!\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!=\!-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\!\Psi_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\!\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!+\!\mathcal{E}_{2},

where the error term
\mathcal{E}_{2}
is defined in terms of the Taylor residuum as

(3.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}:=\Big\langle\!\Psi_{\alpha},\left(\xi_{p}(Y_{n})+\frac{1}{2}\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})-p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\right)L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\!\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}.

We are going to show in Lemma
8.3
that
\mathcal{E}_{2}
is small compared to
\frac{\sqrt{\Lambda}|p|^{3}}{\alpha^{6}}
, i.e. as long as the cut-off parameter
\Lambda
is chosen small compared to
\frac{\alpha^{4}}{|p|^{2}}
,
\mathcal{E}_{2}
is of the order
o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right)
.

In order to provide an asymptotically correct computation of the first contribution on the right hand side of Eq. (
3.22
)

(3.24)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\!\Psi_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\!\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)},

it will be essential to make use of the concept of Bose-Einstein condensation, see
[
4
,
5
]
in the context of the Fröhlich polaron, which tells us that low energy states
\Phi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}
of the (cut-off) Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}^{K}
, see Eq. (
2.8
), are close, in a suitable sense, to a superposition of the tensor products with coherent states
\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}_{z}\otimes\Xi_{\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}_{z}}
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined in Eq. (
2.11
). Furthermore, in case the (regularized) median is highly localized at the origin
0\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the low energy state
\Phi
is even close to the single tensor product
\psi^{\mathrm{Pek}}\otimes\Xi_{\varphi^{\mathrm{Pek}}}
, see
[
4
]
. Unfortunately, we cannot directly apply this result for
\Psi_{\alpha}
, since
\Psi_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{F}
is only an element of the fiber Hilbert space
\mathcal{F}
. In the following we want to lift
\Psi_{\alpha}
to a state
\Phi_{\alpha}\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}
on the full Hilbert space, in such a way that the (regularized) median is localized around the origin. Given the parameter

(3.25)

\displaystyle\eta:=\alpha^{-\beta},

where
\frac{2}{29}<\beta<\frac{3}{4}
is fixed, representing the sharpness of the localization, let us recall the definition of

\displaystyle\tau_{\eta}(y)=\eta^{-\frac{3}{2}}\tau\!\left(\eta^{-1}y\right),

from Subsection
2.4
, where
\tau:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow[0,1]
is smooth with support in
\left\{y:|y|\leq\frac{1}{3}\right\}
satisfying
\int\tau(y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y=1
. Then we define the state
\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}(x;Y_{n}):=\mu_{\alpha}^{-1}\tau_{\eta}\!\left(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})+x\right)F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n}),

where
\mu_{\alpha}\in(0,1]
is chosen such that
\|\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}\|=1
, as well as the state
\Phi_{\alpha}\in L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)\otimes\mathcal{F}
via the map
\mathcal{T}
from Eq. (
2.7
) as

(3.27)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\alpha}(x;Y_{n}):=\left(\mathcal{T}\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}\right)\!(x;Y_{n})=\mu_{\alpha}^{-1}\tau_{\eta}\!\left(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\right)F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\Psi_{\alpha}(y_{1}-x_{1},\dots,y_{n}-x).

It will be the content of Lemma
9.1
to show that
\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}
is a low energy state of the (cut-off) Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}^{K}_{\frac{1}{i}\nabla}
defined in Eq. (
2.9
), or equivalently
\Phi_{\alpha}
is a low energy state of the (cut-off) Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}^{K}
defined in Eq. (
2.8
). Furthermore, we clearly have for
\Phi_{\alpha}
that the regularized median
m_{\eta}
is localized around the origin on a length scale of the order
\eta
. Using the fact that we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})+x)^{2}\mathrm{d}x=1
for all
Y_{n}
, we can express the term in Eq. (
3.24
) as

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\!\Psi_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\!\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\!\Psi_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})+x)^{2}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\Psi_{\alpha}\!\right\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}

\displaystyle=\!-\frac{1}{2}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!\!\left\langle\!\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\!+\!x)\Psi_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)\,\Psi_{\alpha}\!\right\rangle_{\!\!\!L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.28)

\displaystyle=\!-\frac{1}{2}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!\left\langle\!\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)\Psi_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)\,\Psi_{\alpha}\!\right\rangle_{\!\!\!L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\!\Psi_{\alpha},\!\varphi_{\eta}(Y_{n})\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\!\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)},

where we have used the commutator relation
L(v^{\Lambda})\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)=\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\!+\!x)L(v^{\Lambda})
and defined
\varphi_{\eta}
as

(3.29)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\eta}(Y_{n}):

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left[\tau_{\eta}\!\left(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})+x\right)-\tau_{\eta}\!\left(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})+x\right)\right]\tau_{\eta}\!\left(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})+x\right)\mathrm{d}x.

By Lemma
8.3
, the residual term
\mathcal{E}_{3}
, defined as

(3.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}:=-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\!\Psi_{\alpha},\!\varphi_{\eta}(Y_{n})\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\Psi_{\alpha}\!\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)},

is of the order
\frac{\sqrt{\Lambda}|p|^{2}}{\eta^{4}\alpha^{8}}
, and therefore
o\!\left(\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}\right)
as long as
\eta
is large compared to
\alpha^{-1}\Lambda^{\frac{1}{8}}
. Finally, in order to extract the essential contribution from the first term on the right hand side of Eq. (
3.28
), recall that
\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})
is mostly supported on configurations
Y_{n}
such that
F(\rho_{Y_{n}})=1
, for which we have

\displaystyle\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)\Psi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\alpha}\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}(x;Y_{n}),

\displaystyle p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})

\displaystyle=p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}}).

Based on this observation we are going to decompose the first term in Eq. (
3.28
) as

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!\left\langle\!\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)\Psi_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)\,\Psi_{\alpha}\!\right\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ =-\frac{\mu_{\alpha}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!\left\langle\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}(x;\cdot),\!\left[p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}(x;\cdot)\right\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{E}_{4}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ =-\frac{\mu_{\alpha}^{2}}{2}\left\langle\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}+\mathcal{E}_{4}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ =-\frac{\mu_{\alpha}^{2}}{2}\left\langle\Phi_{\alpha},\!\left[p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\!L(v^{\Lambda}_{x})\Phi_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}+\mathcal{E}_{4},

where we consider
\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha}(x;\cdot)
as a state of
\mathcal{F}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{4}:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!\Big\langle\tau_{\eta}\!\left(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})+x\right)\Psi_{\alpha},\Big(F(\rho_{Y_{n}})F(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\left[p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle-\left[p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right]^{2}\Big)\!L(v^{\Lambda})\tau_{\eta}\!\left(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})+x\right)\Psi_{\alpha}(Y_{n})\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.31)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ =-\frac{1}{2}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!\Big\langle\!\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)\Psi_{\alpha},\!\left(F(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!F(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n}})p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!-\!p\!\cdot\!G(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})p\!\cdot\!\mathcal{B}(\rho_{Y_{n-1}})\right)\!L(v^{\Lambda})\tau_{\eta}(m_{\eta}(\rho_{Y_{n}})\!+\!x)\,\Psi_{\alpha}\!\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\!\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bigcup}\mathbb{R}^{3n}\right)}\!\mathrm{d}x.

We going to confirm in Lemma
8.4
that the term
\mathcal{E}_{4}
is of the order
\frac{\sqrt{\Lambda}|p|^{2}}{\alpha^{6}}
, and therefore small compared to
\alpha^{-4}|p|^{2}
for
\Lambda
small compared to
\alpha^{4}
.
